### 🤖 AI Intelligence Summary
> Amir Chand Jagdish Kumar (Exports) Limited reported strong financial growth in FY2025-26, with significant increases in revenue and profit, alongside improved EBITDA margins. A material impact is the successful April 2026 IPO, which raised ₹440.00 crore and drastically strengthened the balance sheet by reducing the debt-to-equity ratio from 2.07x to 0.82x, primarily for working capital. The company is strategically pivoting from an export-led rice business to a diversified domestic FMCG platform, aiming for broader product portfolio and distribution, which helps mitigate inherent agricultural and market competition risks for future growth.

Disclosure Requirements) Regulations, 2015, please take note that the 23rd (Twenty Third) Annual 
General Meeting (“AGM”) of the Members of the Company is scheduled to be held on Tuesday, 
September 29, 2026 at 11:00 a.m. (IST) through Video Conferencing ('VC') / Other Audio-Visual 
Means ('OAVM') facility in compliance with the applicable provisions of the Companies Act, 2013 and 
rules framed thereunder read with Circulars issued by the Ministry of Corporate Affairs (“MCA”) and 
the Securities and Exchange Board of India (“SEBI”) in this regard, from time to time. 
 
The Notice along with the Annual Report for the financial year 2025-26 is being sent only through 
electronic mode to all the members of the Company, whose e-mail addresses are registered with the 
Company/Depository(ies)/Registrar & Share Transfer Agent (“RTA”) and is also available on the 
website of the Company at https://www.aeroplanerice.com/. 
 
Further, in accordance with regulation 36 of the SEBI Listing Regulations, a letter providing the 
weblink for accessing the Notice along with Annual Report for the financial year 2025-26 is being 
sent to all those members who have not registered their email addresses with the 
Company/Depository Participant(s)/RTA.

Strategic Review
Our Products
Rice is the soul of our business, with 
fragrant basmati at its core, driving most 
of our revenue. Alongside basmati, we 
offer speciality varieties such as kolam, 
sona masuri, idli rice, ponni, and brown 
rice, allowing us to cater to a wide 
spectrum of tastes and markets. Our 
FMCG range brings everyday kitchen 
staples to homes, including atta and 
maida, besan, sooji, idli rice flour, instant 
phirni, salt, and sugar. While our rice 
travels across borders, our FMCG 
offerings are crafted especially for Indian 
households.
Basmati occupies a distinctive position in 
the global grain trade. Its Geographical 
Indication recognises its origin in 
designated regions of northern India and 
reinforces the standards associated
with its provenance, quality, and 
characteristics.
Our Journey
Amir Chand Jagdish Kumar (Exports) Limited traces its roots to a humble 
trading venture, M/S Amir Chand Jagdish Kumar, founded by our visionary 
Promoter, Mr. Jagdish Kumar Suri. Guided by a deep knowledge of rice, a 
disciplined approach to sourcing, and relationships built to last, our business 
blossomed from a proprietorship into a dynamic, professionally managed 
food enterprise.
ABOUT COMPANY 
The present Company was incorporated 
in 2003 and acquired the proprietorship's 
assets in 2005, including the Safidon and 
Alipur facilities, now Units II and III. With 
the Aeroplane brand already established 
for nearly two decades, we crossed `500 
crore in revenue in 2008. In 2010, we 
established Unit I at Amritsar with an in-
house turbine plant, expanding its 
www.aeroplanerice.com
07
capacity from 384 to 576 MT per day by 
2013. Our journey took a bold step 
forward in 2022 with the launch of our 
FMCG portfolio, opening doors to a wider 
array of everyday food products.
At every stage, we have stayed true to 
our core values: growing patiently, acting 
responsibly, and earning trust one step
at a time.
For us, quality is cultivated long before 
milling begins. The length of each grain, 
its aroma, texture, and careful ageing are 
the hallmarks of our premium basmati. 
We age our finest grades upto 24 months, 
and our medium grades upto 9 months, 
nurturing the rich flavour, aroma, and 
cooking qualities that define exceptional 
basmati.

Strategic Review
An Integrated Business
Our true strength comes from how deeply 
we are woven into the basmati value 
chain. From sourcing and storing to 
ageing, milling, processing, packaging, 
branding, marketing, and distribution, we 
oversee every step with care.
Our procurement network comprises 325 
registered agents across the mandis of 
Punjab, Haryana, Delhi and Uttar 
Pradesh, supported by our in-house 
procurement team. Many of these 
relationships have been built over 
decades and are founded on consistent 
offtake, fair dealing and timely payments.
In a world where the best paddy is 
secured in a narrow seasonal window, 
and premium lots are quickly claimed, 
trust and relationships are as vital as 
price. Our procurement network stands 
as a lasting pillar of our business.
Our integrated approach ensures that 
every tonne of paddy is put to its best 
use. Husk from milling fuels our boilers 
and powers Unit I, cutting our reliance on 
coal. Bran finds new life in edible oil 
extraction and cattle feed, turning every 
part of the grain into value.
Quality is woven into every stage of our 
process. Our dedicated 14-member 
Quality Assurance team keeps a vigilant 
eye through sampling and inspections, 
ensuring every consignment meets our 
standards before it leaves our doors.
Leadership
Our leadership team combines deep 
industry experience, strategic vision, and 
operational expertise with a strong 
understanding of the Company's 
heritage, markets, and integrated 
business model. Its collective strengths 
span procurement, finance, domestic and 
international sales, production, quality, 
marketing, and administration.
Built on decades of hands-on experience, 
the team brings continuity, 
accountability, and decisive execution, 
while remaining focused on evolving with 
changing markets and consumer needs. 
This balance of institutional knowledge 
and forward-looking leadership provides 
a strong foundation for the Company's 
next phase of growth.
Governance & Responsibility
As a public company, we recognise that 
strong governance is integral to building 
a resilient and trusted institution. Our 
governance framework is supported by 
an experienced and diverse Board, with a 
balanced representation of executive and 
independent perspectives. The Board and 
its committees operate within the 
applicable regulatory framework, with a 
clear focus on oversight, accountability 
and responsible decision-making.
For us, governance extends beyond 
compliance. It is about doing what is right, 
maintaining transparency and building trust 
that endures—with our shareholders and 
every stakeholder who is part of our 
journey.
Responsible Procurement
Responsibility begins at the source. We 
hold the licences required for direct 
procurement from mandis and, where 
applicable, work through registered 
agents. While our procurement agents 
are not engaged through long-term 
contracts, our relationships are built on 
consistent business, transparent 
dealings, and timely settlement of dues.
This philosophy has helped us nurture a 
procurement network rooted in mutual 
trust and unwavering reliability.
Food Safety & Quality
In a food business, trust begins with 
quality and safety. Our facilities operate 
under ISO 22000:2018-compliant food 
safety systems, hold Central FSSAI 
licences, and are HACCP-accredited.
We monitor quality at every step, with 
each consignment undergoing a final 
check before it leaves our facility. 
Certifications set the stage, but it is our 
consistent execution that truly assures 
quality.
Our People
Our people are the heartbeat of our 
company, driving our consistent 
performance. With a non-unionised 
workforce and no labour disputes in the 
past four years, we have built a stable 
and harmonious workplace.
We strive to foster a workplace that is 
stable, respectful, and performance-driven, 
celebrating the contributions of our 
employees across all roles and locations.
A Defining Milestone: Our IPO
For us, the listing is more than a financial 
achievement. It marks a transformation in 
our scale, governance, and sense of 
responsibility.
We now have a broader shareholder base. 
With a wider shareholder base, increased 
visibility, and access to public capital,
we embrace a higher standard of 
accountability and a clear duty to invest 
wisely, strengthen our business, and 
build lasting value. Built over four 
decades. Our ambition now is to build 
upon them with the discipline, 
governance, and institutional strength 
required for the decades ahead.
Quality is woven into every stage of our process.
Our dedicated 14-member Quality Assurance team
keeps a vigilant eye through sampling and inspections, 
ensuring every consignment meets our standards 
before it leaves our doors.
Amir Chand Jagdish Kumar (Exports) Limited

Strategic Review
CMD’S MESSAGE
Dear Shareholders,
It is my privilege to address you for the first time as 
shareholders of Amir Chand Jagdish Kumar (Exports) 
Limited following the Company's transition to a 
publicly listed enterprise.
From a business established more than four decades 
ago as a family enterprise, we have evolved into an 
integrated rice business with a presence across 
domestic and international markets. While the scale, 
technology and operating environment have changed 
considerably over the years, our fundamental commitment
to quality, reliability and customer relationships has
remained consistent. Today, that same principle forms
the foundation of our next phase of growth.
Profit after tax increased to `103.30 crore 
from `60.82 crore.
The key achievement was not merely 
revenue growth, but the improvement in 
the quality of earnings. Better utilisation 
of our existing milling infrastructure, a 
higher contribution from branded 
domestic sales, and disciplined 
procurement planning supported margin 
expansion.
Capacity utilisation improved from 
50.46% to 61.06%. With significant 
capacity still available, we see 
meaningful potential to grow volumes 
and improve operating leverage without 
immediately requiring large investments 
in additional manufacturing capacity.
A More Balanced Business
Building a Diversified, Full-Fledged FMCG 
Platform
Our business has undergone a significant 
transformation over the past three years, 
with the Company evolving from an 
export-led business towards a diversified, 
consumer-focused FMCG platform. While 
approximately 70% of our revenue was 
historically derived from exports, exports 
contributed approximately one-third of 
our revenue in FY2025–26. This evolution 
reflects our deliberate strategy to build a 
stronger domestic consumer franchise 
while continuing to leverage our 
established international presence.
We see a significant opportunity in India's 
branded packaged food and FMCG 
market, supported by rising household 
incomes, premiumisation, changing 
consumption preferences, organised 
retail, e-commerce and the rapid adoption 
of quick commerce. We are therefore 
investing in our domestic sales and 
distribution capabilities and expanding 
our portfolio across multiple everyday-
consumption categories, with the 
www.aeroplanerice.com
12
A Defining Year
FY2025–26 was transformational for the Company. In 
March 2026, we completed our Initial Public Offering 
and raised `440.00 crore of fresh capital. On 2 April 
2026, our equity shares were listed on the BSE and 
NSE.
This transition brings greater access to capital, but 
more importantly, greater accountability. As a listed 
company, we recognise our responsibility towards 
shareholders, employees, customers, business 
partners and all other stakeholders. Transparency, 
governance, disciplined capital allocation, and 
sustainable value creation will remain central to how 
we operate.
Stronger Profitability
The Company delivered a healthy improvement in 
profitability during the year.
Revenue from operations increased to `2,287.14 crore 
from `2,001.65 crore in FY2024–25. EBITDA grew 
significantly to `225.22 crore from `163.65 crore, with 
the EBITDA margin improving from 8.18% to 9.85%.

Strategic Review
objective of building Aeroplane as a 
recognised, multi-category FMCG brand.
Our strategy is built around leveraging 
our existing sales, distribution, sourcing 
and logistics infrastructure to introduce 
and scale additional FMCG categories. 
This provides us with an opportunity to 
increase our presence across consumer 
households, strengthen cross-selling 
across our portfolio and build a more 
scalable and diversified revenue base. 
The domestic business also offers 
structural advantages, including shorter 
supply chains, lower freight costs and 
potentially faster working-capital cycles.
At the same time, our international 
business remains an important pillar of 
our growth strategy. We currently serve 
38 countries across four continents and 
remain a Three-Star Export House. The 
Middle East continues to be a key export 
market, supported by longstanding 
customer relationships and repeat 
business. Going forward, our objective is 
not to choose between domestic and 
international markets, but to build a 
balanced, geographically diversified 
FMCG business with multiple channels, 
categories and markets.
Our aspiration is to evolve into a full-
fledged FMCG player with a strong 
domestic consumer franchise, a growing 
international footprint and a broad 
portfolio of everyday-consumption 
products, supported by an increasingly 
professional sales organisation, 
diversified distribution channels and an 
integrated supply-chain ecosystem.
Strengthening the Balance Sheet
A significant portion of the IPO proceeds 
is being deployed towards working 
capital. This is central to the economics 
of the basmati rice business. Basmati 
paddy is largely procured between 
September and January, requiring 
substantial liquidity during the 
procurement season. Premium varieties 
also require ageing for approximately 12 
to 24 months before milling and packing. 
Managing this cycle efficiently is 
therefore critical to both quality and 
profitability.
Historically, this requirement was funded 
significantly through borrowings. Our 
debt-to-equity ratio has now improved to 
0.82x from 2.07x a year earlier. A stronger 
balance sheet provides greater flexibility 
in procurement and reduces our 
dependence on short-term borrowing. It 
enables us to take a longer-term view of 
inventory and procurement decisions 
while strengthening the business's 
overall financial resilience.
Our Strategic Priorities
Our priorities for the coming years are 
focused and clear.
Expand distribution.
We have 436 distributors and aim to 
increase this to more than 700 by FY28, 
with a strong focus on Tier-3 and Tier-4 
markets where our penetration remains 
relatively low.
Build stronger brands.
Aeroplane has developed significant 
consumer recognition over the years with 
relatively modest advertising investment. 
However, consumer behaviour is 
changing rapidly. Modern retail and 
digital commerce require brands to 
communicate directly with consumers 
and compete for attention at the point of 
purchase.
We therefore intend to invest more 
deliberately in brand building, consumer 
communication and digital channels 
while continuing to strengthen the equity 
of our established brands.
Expand beyond rice.
Rice will remain our core category, but we 
see a substantial opportunity to build a 
broader food portfolio. Variety of 
consumer staples currently account for a 
small portion of our business. We intend 
to expand into, ghee, daliya, poha  
pulses, and edible oils selected value-
added products. Our existing sourcing 
capabilities, distribution network and 
brand presence provide a strong platform 
for this expansion.
We have 436 distributors 
and aim to increase this to 
more than 700 by FY28, 
with a strong focus on 
Tier-3 and Tier-4 markets 
where our penetration 
remains relatively low.

Looking Ahead
The listing of Amir Chand Jagdish Kumar 
(Exports) Limited marks the beginning of 
a new phase—not the culmination of our 
journey.
We intend to significantly strengthen and 
scale our sales organisation as we 
pursue our growth ambitions in the FMCG 
sector. As part of this initiative, we are 
actively identifying and attracting 
experienced sales professionals with 
relevant experience across leading FMCG 
companies, with a focus on 
strengthening our capabilities across key 
markets and channels.
To support this talent acquisition strategy, 
we have onboarded Metamorph, a 
specialised executive search and head-
hunting agency, to assist us in identifying 
high-quality sales leadership and 
commercial talent. We believe that building 
a strong, professionally managed sales 
organisation will enhance our market 
penetration, strengthen channel 
relationships and provide the capabilities 
required to support our next phase of 
growth.
To pursue our goal of becoming a full-
fledged FMCG player, we have 
onboarded Rashmika Mandanna as a 
brand ambassador to strengthen our 
brand visibility and connect with a wider 
and younger consumer base.
Her association is expected to enhance 
brand recall, consumer engagement and 
awareness, supporting our strategy of 
building Aeroplane into a strong and 
aspirational FMCG brand across domestic 
and international markets.
We enter this phase with recognised 
brands, established relationships, a 
diversified market presence, a growing 
domestic business, a stronger balance 
sheet and significant headroom within 
our existing infrastructure.
Our priorities are clear: grow responsibly, 
strengthen profitability, improve asset 
utilisation, expand our brands and 
product portfolio, and maintain financial 
discipline.
We look forward to building the next 
chapter of Amir Chand Jagdish Kumar 
(Exports) Limited with the same 
discipline and commitment that have 
guided us through the past four decades.
Thank you for your trust and continued 
support.

Utilise existing capacity.
We will focus on improving utilisation, 
productivity and returns from our current 
manufacturing infrastructure before 
undertaking significant capacity 
expansion. There is considerable 
headroom within our existing assets.
Managing Risk with Discipline
The fundamentals of our industry also 
bring inherent risks. Our business is 
linked to a single annual agricultural 
harvest. It is therefore exposed to 
weather conditions, crop yields, paddy 
prices, government policies, export 
regulations, international import 
restrictions and foreign exchange 
movements.
Competition remains intense in several 
domestic and international markets, with 
pricing pressure that can impact margins. 
Our procurement model also depends on 
established agents and relationships 
rather than long-term contractual 
arrangements.
To pursue our goal of 
becoming a full-fledged 
FMCG player, we have 
onboarded Rashmika 
Mandanna as a brand 
ambassador to strengthen 
our brand visibility and 
connect with a wider and 
younger consumer base.

Aeroplane is a portfolio of over forty sub-brands, each aligned with a specific grain grade, 
price point, and customer segment. A household purchasing aged premium basmati for 
weddings and caterers buying bulk sacks for daily service have distinct needs, which we 
address through tailored packaging. Grade determines the tier, which in turn defines the 
brand, packaging, price, and distribution channel. This approach enables us to compete 
across all price segments while maintaining the integrity of our premium offerings.
We hold 100 registered trademarks—70 in India and 30 across 26 countries in Europe, Asia, 
and Africa—as well as 22 copyrights in India. Our brand's strength is evident in our 
consistent sales, achieved with advertising and marketing expenses below 0.5% of total 
costs, reflecting decades of reliable quality.
Going forward, modern trade, e-commerce, and quick commerce reflect evolving 
consumer expectations, requiring packaging to stand out on the shelf. We will invest 
strategically in media visibility while maintaining transparency in our messaging.

The Domestic Market
India has become our largest market, now accounting for 67% of our operating revenue. 
Domestic revenue has grown at a compound annual rate of about 46.63% from FY23 to 
FY25, making it the fastest-growing part of our business and a key focus for us. We 
operate in major cities and most tier-1 and tier-2 cities through three main channels.
International Markets
We are a Three Star Export House 
recognised by the Ministry of Commerce 
and Industry, with exports to 38 countries 
on four continents through 53 
distributors.
The Middle East remains our main export 
region, accounting for 12% of our 
operating revenue. In this region, Indian 
basmati is a daily staple, not just a 
speciality import, and our long-term 
relationships are supported by repeat 
orders. Australia, South Africa, Europe, 
Azerbaijan, and other markets 
collectively accounted for 21% of our 
exports, and their share has grown as we 
have expanded.
Our mill registrations for the United 
States and China, along with EIC approval 
at Unit II, allow us to serve markets with 
strict entry requirements.
MARKET COVERAGE 
DOMESTIC + INTERNATIONAL + INSTITUTIONAL
General trade
This is our biggest channel, supported by 
major distributors who supply both retail 
outlets and wholesale agents. We 
carefully select our distributors based on 
area coverage, sales skills, financial and 
operational history, reputation, and credit 
practices. Many of these partnerships 
have lasted for decades, even without 
formal contracts, which allows us to keep 
finished stock with distributors and helps 
us manage our inventory. 
Modern trade
We work with both national and regional 
retail chains, supporting these 
partnerships with in-store promotions 
and joint activities. Our premium 
products compete most directly in this 
channel, where packaging, product 
quality, and pricing play the biggest roles 
in attracting customers.
E-commerce and quick commerce
We have partnered with third-party 
marketplaces and quick-commerce

Institutional and Direct Sales

and packed for large-scale cooking and

institutional buying cycles.

Discussion & Analysis
GLOBAL ECONOMIC OVERVIEW
Trade barriers stayed high through the year, and policy uncertainty with them. Yet the global economy held up better 
than most forecasts had allowed for, sustained by continued technology investment, loose financial conditions by 
historical standards, and fiscal and monetary support that the major economies had not yet begun to withdraw.
That balance broke in February 2026, when conflict began in the Middle East. It now stands as the single largest 
downside risk in the global outlook. Its effects ripple through commodity markets, inflation expectations, and financial 
conditions, and they show up in practice as higher energy and food prices, choppier markets, and currency pressure on 
emerging economies that import commodities.
Against that backdrop, aggregate inflation moved little, though the regional picture diverged. Core personal consumption 
expenditure inflation in the United States stayed above target. Bond yields firmed, and equity markets softened after the 
conflict began, modestly tightening financial conditions, although they remain accommodative by historical standards. 
The dollar did what it usually does in such conditions and strengthened. Gold turned volatile.
Public finances are stretched in several economies, both advanced and emerging. Against that pressure, conflict-related 
spending has been added to measures that protect vulnerable households from commodity price increases, at exactly 
the point when higher financing costs and slower activity are eroding revenue. Deficits have widened accordingly.
Commodity markets have been unsettled throughout. In turn, supply and transportation disruptions have driven up 
energy prices. Natural gas remains structurally exposed. Food prices face pressure from two directions at once: input 
costs and logistics.
GLOBAL ECONOMIC OUTLOOK
Growth is expected to slow. Global GDP is projected at 3.1 per cent in 2026 and 3.2 per cent in 2027, against roughly 
3.4 per cent in 2025. Geopolitical tension, tighter trade conditions and structural constraints account for most of the 
slowdown.

Advanced economies should manage 1.8 per cent in 2026 and 1.7 per cent in 2027, with the United States the most 
resilient of the group, helped by fiscal measures and the delayed benefit of earlier monetary easing. The Euro area 
is likely to stay flat, held back by energy costs and a weak manufacturing base. Overall, advanced economies should 
remain contained, with net energy importers the main exception.
Emerging market and developing economies are projected at 3.9 per cent in 2026, recovering to 4.2 per cent in 2027. 
Their exposure is greater: commodity prices, currency depreciation, and external financing conditions all affect them 
more directly, and the conflict will be felt more sharply here, especially by commodity importers.
World trade will slow more sharply than output, from 
5.1 per cent in 2025 to 2.8 per cent in 2026, before 
recovering to 3.8 per cent in 2027. Trade tensions, tariffs 
and supply chain adjustment explain the fall. Goods 
and services trade should both soften, with services 
proving the more durable of the two.
Considerable uncertainty attaches to all of this. The 
duration and intensity of the Middle East conflict will do 
more than any other variable to determine the outcome. 
The risks are sustained commodity prices, a return of 
inflation, tighter financial conditions and widening 
fiscal gaps. Set against them are supportive policy and 
the structural resilience of the larger economies, which 
should keep global growth positive over the medium 
term.
Source: World Economic Outlook, April 2026 — Global 
Economy in the Shadow of War.
 
INDIAN ECONOMY OVERVIEW
India held its position as the fastest-growing major 
economy in FY26, with GDP growth accelerating to 7.6 
per cent from 7.1 per cent the previous year. The central 
story was that manufacturing and services drove the 
expansion, while agriculture slowed against a high 
base.
Manufacturing strengthened on the back of the 
automobile and fast-moving consumer goods sectors. 
Services remained the principal engine, with firm 
urban demand evident in retail trade, hospitality, 
transportation, and real estate, while exports of 
financial and professional services remained buoyant. 
This reinforced the broader point that growth was led 
by industry and services rather than agriculture, which 
moderated because foodgrain yields could not repeat 
the gains of an unusually strong preceding harvest.
Money 
became 
considerably 
cheaper. 
Between 
February and December 2025, the Reserve Bank cut the 
policy repo rate by a cumulative 125 basis points, to 5.25 
per cent. At the February 2026 review, it maintained a 
neutral stance, citing a favourable growth outlook and 
benign inflation, and stressed that future action would 
depend on the data. This underscored that policy 
remained supportive, but data-dependent.
Inflation gave little trouble. Headline inflation averaged 
1.9 per cent between April 2025 and February 2026, well 
below the 4.7 per cent of the year before. Falling food 
prices did most of the work, helped by strong agricultural 
output, comfortable cereal stocks and reduced import 
duties. Wholesale price inflation came in at 0.4 per 
cent as food and fuel prices eased. Meanwhile, core 
inflation moved in the opposite direction, averaging 
4.1 per cent due to higher commodity prices. However, 
healthcare, education and transport stayed stable, and 
core inflation eased towards the end of the period. The 
overall point was a benign inflation backdrop.
Households spent. Private consumption grew 7.7 per 
cent, helped by rising disposable incomes, low inflation,

personal income tax relief and the rationalisation of 
GST rates. Consumer sentiment improved alongside 
spending patterns, reinforcing domestic demand as a 
key driver of growth.
On trade, merchandise exports between April and 
February reached USD 402.93 billion, up 1.84 per cent, 
while imports rose 8.53 per cent to USD 713.53 billion. 
Including services, total exports grew 5.8 per cent to USD 
790.86 billion. Leading the way were engineering goods, 
petroleum products, electronics, pharmaceuticals, 
gems and jewellery, textiles and agricultural products, 
into the United States, the UAE, China, Europe and 
Southeast Asia. Trade therefore remained active, even 
as imports outpaced merchandise exports.
Geopolitical tension complicated the picture. Disruption 
to the Red Sea and the Strait of Hormuz raised freight 
costs and insurance premiums and lengthened transit 
times. India’s export sector absorbed it, helped by a 
diversified base and competitiveness across sectors. 
For an exporter of basmati to the Gulf, however, the 
disruption was not a general condition of trade but a 
specific cost, and its effect on this Company is set out 
later in this discussion. The implication is that external 
shocks added cost, but did not alter the broader export 
story.
On trade, merchandise exports between April and February reached 
USD 402.93 billion, up 1.84 per cent, while imports rose 8.53 per cent 
to USD 713.53 billion. Including services, total exports grew 5.8 per 
cent to USD 790.86 billion.
INDIAN ECONOMY OUTLOOK
Growth should moderate to 6.6 per cent in FY27, 
with global uncertainty accounting for much of the 
slowdown and the Middle East conflict and its effect on 
energy markets accounting for the rest.
Higher crude prices and continued disruptions to energy 
supply chains will add to inflation and erode household 
disposable income, slowing private consumption. 
GST rate reductions should absorb part of the blow. 
Government consumption should also slow, as subsidy 
requirements for cooking gas and fertilisers will take up 
fiscal room that would otherwise be available.
Externally, trade uncertainty and logistical disruptions 
remain challenges to export growth. India’s diversified 
export base, its strength in services exports, and its 
continuing structural reforms should help it hold up 
against them.
The short-term headwinds are real, but so are the 
underlying strengths: stable inflation, active monetary 
policy, and firm domestic demand. Together, they point 
to an economy capable of sustaining steady growth 
over the medium term.
Source: India Remains Among the Fastest-Growing 
Economies Even As Growth Slows Amid Middle East 
Conflict; Outlook Vulnerable to Risks and Uncertainty.
 
INDUSTRY REVIEW
Consumer demand held firm, consumption patterns 
continued to evolve, and digital transformation 
progressed quickly. Together, these forces are driving 
India’s FMCG sector. Projections put it at a compound 
annual rate of 17.33 per cent between FY26 and FY30, 
taking the market to an estimated USD 642.87 billion.
Urban demand outpaced rural demand during FY26. 
Urban FMCG volumes grew 4.6 per cent, while rural 
growth eased to 3.6 per cent, reflecting stronger 
discretionary spending and premiumisation in cities. 
Even so, rural India remains the larger long-term 
opportunity. It has compounded at 11 per cent over five 
years, and both affordable and premium categories are 
finding buyers there.
Retail formats and digital channels are reshaping how 
the sector grows. E-commerce now accounts for 17 per 
cent of FMCG consumption among affluent consumers, 
with internet subscriptions past one billion in 2025. 
Modern trade is growing at 19.5 per cent, while rural 
markets are recovering steadily.
Quick commerce is the disruption. The segment is 
expected to grow 75 to 100 per cent a year. It may reach 
USD 10 billion by 2029, having penetrated only around 7 
per cent of an addressable market estimated at USD 45 
billion. The major platforms recorded 50 to 100 per cent 
year-on-year growth in FY25, most of it in large urban 
clusters rather than in rural markets. FMCG companies 
are responding by rebuilding supply chains, investing in 
last-mile delivery and improving logistics.
Direct-to-consumer and direct-selling models are 
changing the picture, too. India’s D2C market was worth 
USD 80 billion in 2024 and is projected to exceed USD 
100 billion in 2025, as consumers gravitate towards 
personalised products and digital-first brands. But 
the category has become crowded. Product launches 
have risen by 1.8 times, yet only around 4 per cent 
achieve meaningful penetration, placing a premium on 
differentiation and brand strength.
Food processing, which accounts for 32 per cent of the 
total food market, reached USD 354.5 billion in FY24 
and is projected to reach USD 535 billion by FY26. 
Agricultural and processed food exports totalled USD 
49.4 billion in FY25, with processed foods accounting 
for a steadily larger share. The Production Linked 
Incentive scheme continues to strengthen the sector.
The sector’s fundamentals remain sound: favourable 
demographics, 
rising 
incomes, 
digital 
adoption 
and policy support. These forces should carry the 
sector forward, at home and abroad, even as inflation 
and shifting consumption shape the near term. 
Premiumisation, innovation and wider distribution 
remain the key drivers.
Source: Indian FMCG Industry Analysis, IBEF.
INDUSTRY REVIEW
Basmati Industry
Consumers continue to move towards premium 
aromatic long-grain rice, and basmati continues to 
reach new markets. The central thesis is clear: the 
global basmati market is expanding steadily, valued at 
approximately USD 11.36 billion in 2025 and projected 
to grow at around 5.0 per cent per year between 2026 
and 2035, reaching an estimated USD 18.50 billion. Two 
things underpin that: a widening appreciation for high-
quality rice across North America, Europe, the Middle 
East, and Africa, and the spread of organised retail and 
e-commerce, which has put branded basmati within 
reach of consumers who once had access only to loose 
grain.
In particular, demand looks strongest in the Middle 
East and Africa, where growth of roughly 5.5 per cent is 
expected over the forecast period. Aromatic rice holds 
deep cultural significance in Saudi Arabia, Iran, Iraq, 
and the UAE, and rising incomes and population growth 
across the GCC continue to drive consumption. Saudi 
Arabia, in particular, takes a substantial share of India’s 
basmati exports.
Within end users, demand from HoReCa and institutions 
should continue to grow. Biryani, pilaf and other 
basmati-based dishes have travelled well, and airline 
catering, healthcare and corporate food services 
are adding further institutional volume for premium 
varieties.
India dominates the trade, supplying somewhere 
between 65 and 70 per cent of global export volumes. 
Its position rests on a large GI-certified cultivation area, 
established export infrastructure and internationally 
recognised brands. Product range is a strength as 
well: 45 varieties are notified under the Seeds Act, 
1966, running from traditional Basmati 370 and Taraori 
through to high-yielding modern varieties such as Pusa 
Basmati 1121 and Pusa Basmati 1509.
Cultivation is concentrated in northern India, in Punjab, 
Haryana, Uttar Pradesh, Uttarakhand and parts of 
Jammu and Kashmir. Agro-climatic conditions, fertile 
alluvial soil, and strong agricultural capacity all favour 
the crop there. At the same time, developed irrigation 
systems and policy support have kept production 
consistent enough to meet both domestic consumption 
and export demand.
A structural shift towards branding is underway, and 
it follows consumer preference for packaged, quality-
assured products. This shift reinforces the market’s 
central growth story: companies are investing heavily

in brand building, digital marketing and consumer 
engagement to hold customers and capture more value. 
The shift from loose to branded rice is fastest in urban 
and semi-urban markets, accelerating category growth.
Technology is redefining the sector on two fronts. 
It supports the market’s central shift by making 
packaged rice easier to trust and differentiate. On 
the consumer side, participants are adopting AI-
driven retail experiences, smart packaging, and QR-
based authentication and traceability, all of which 
build confidence and help distinguish one pack from 
another. On the export side, vacuum-sealed, resealable 
packaging protects quality and shelf life during long 
transit.
Finally, crop development is improving the economics 
of cultivation itself. New varieties such as Pusa Basmati 
1979 and Pusa Basmati 1985 are herbicide-tolerant and 
suited to Direct Seeded Rice. They use less water and 
less labour and yield better, which improves the return 
to the grower and, over time, the reliability of supply to 
the mill.
Indian Basmati Rice Outlook
Three forces continue to support the sector: export 
demand, premiumisation at home and government 
policy. Together, they show why the sector’s outlook 
remains positive. The domestic basmati market was 
valued at approximately INR 499.5 billion in 2025 and 
is projected to reach INR 541.8 billion by 2034. The 
overall India rice market (all types) is estimated at USD 
49.32 billion in 2026, growing from USD 47.1 billion in 
2025 toward USD 62.09 billion by 2031 at a 4.72% CAGR( 
modor intelligence)
India should retain its leadership because its cultivation 
base, export expertise and established global supply 
chains are difficult to replicate. International demand 
India dominates the trade, 
supplying somewhere between 
65 and 70 per cent of global 
export volumes. Its position rests 
on a large GI-certified cultivation 
area, established export 
infrastructure and internationally 
recognised brands.
for premium rice from the Middle East, Europe, and 
North America continues to grow, while new markets 
are opening and should reduce concentration risk over 
the medium term.
Consumer preference is moving towards branded, 
hygienically packaged and traceable food. Rising 
incomes, urbanisation and changing consumption 
habits are together turning a staple purchase into a 
premium one, reinforcing the sector’s core growth 
driver.
Technology should further strengthen the outlook by 
expanding reach and improving efficiency. Investment 
in supply chain digitisation, smart packaging and online 
distribution is supporting premium basmati sales, and 
e-commerce and modern retail will be central to that 
growth.
On the production side, better seed varieties, water-
efficient techniques and mechanisation should lift 
yields while reducing resource intensity. Government 
initiatives on rice exports, agricultural innovation, and 
farmer incomes provide further support to the sector’s 
outlook.
Input costs, climate variability and geopolitics all 
remain live challenges. Even so, strong structural 
fundamentals and India’s dominant position in trade 
support continued growth and value creation over the 
medium- to long-term.
Sources: Basmati Rice Market Size, Share, Trends & 
Forecast 2035; India Basmati Rice Market Size, Demand 
& Outlook, 2034; Indian Organic Basmati Rice — 
Varieties, Quality & Production Insights.
INDUSTRY REVIEW
Packaged Food Industry
Macroeconomic 
conditions, 
changing 
consumer 
preferences, and a rapidly expanding retail and 
distribution network have kept the Indian packaged 
food industry growing strongly. The central story 
is steady expansion, with the market valued at 
approximately USD 129.18 billion in 2025 and projected 
to reach USD 238.83 billion by 2034, at a compound 
annual growth rate of 6.24 per cent between 2026 and 
2034. Rising disposable incomes, rapid urbanisation, 
more dual-income households and a growing appetite 
for convenience explain most of that. Policy has helped 
as well: the Production Linked Incentive Scheme for 
Food Processing Industries had drawn cumulative 
investment of about INR 9,207 crore by December 2025, 
encouraging both capacity expansion and innovation.
Beyond overall growth, the more interesting shift is in 
who is buying. Packaged food has moved from a metro 
category to a household staple across tier 2 and tier 3 
markets. Better cold chain, improved last-mile logistics 
and wider availability through modern retail and digital 
channels have made the difference. Consumers are 
moving from loose and unbranded products to branded, 
quality-assured alternatives, and rising incomes, 
greater awareness of hygiene and safety, and changing 
lifestyles are all pushing in the same direction.
Urbanisation remains the long-term driver. India’s 
urban population is projected at roughly 675 million 
by 2035, which will keep demand for branded and 
convenience-oriented food rising across metros and 
emerging cities alike. At the same time, supermarkets, 
hypermarkets, online grocery and quick commerce 
are all improving accessibility and widening product 
discovery. Organised retail, led by supermarkets and 
hypermarkets, held a 52.8 per cent share in 2025, while 
convenience stores, at 17.6 per cent, serve impulse and 
on-the-go purchases.
Quick commerce has once again changed consumption 
patterns. Delivery within ten to thirty minutes is 
now standard in urban markets, and the channel has 
produced innovations of its own: curated pack sizes, 
exclusive SKUs, bundled offerings and logistics-friendly 
packaging. Purchase frequency has risen, and so has 
impulse buying.
Health and wellness is reshaping preferences. Low-
sugar, high-protein, and high-fibre options are gaining 
ground, and premium segments, including fortified, 
plant-based, and millet-based products, are growing 
quickly. In response, manufacturers are answering with 
product innovation and health-focused sub-brands. 
Alongside this, portfolio regionalisation is becoming 
important, with companies introducing localised flavours 
and culturally specific offerings to widen their reach.
Technology is improving both product and supply chain. 
HPP, UHT and aseptic packaging extend shelf life and 
enable clean-label products, while AI-driven demand 
forecasting and real-time cold-chain monitoring reduce 
wastage and sharpen operations. Packaging innovation 
matters commercially too: resealable and vacuum-
sealed formats, better labelling and traceability features 
protect freshness, improve convenience and build the 
consumer trust that premiumisation depends on.
The industry is undergoing a genuine transformation, with 
steady expansion as the central theme. Consumption is 
spreading geographically, premiumisation is advancing, 
channels are being reinvented, and health trends are 
being met. Demographics, policy, and technology all 
point in the same direction, and the opportunities for 
value creation run right along the chain.
Sources: Packaged Food Market in India — Size, Industry 
Trends 2034; Processed Foods, APEDA.

COMPANY OVERVIEW
About ACJKEL
The business began as a partnership concern which 
later became proprietorship concern, M/s. Amir Chand 
Jagdish Kumar, established by Jagdish Kumar Suri. Rice 
was bought in the mandis of northern India, milled, and 
sold under a name that traders came to recognise. That 
name was Aeroplane, and it has been on our packs for 
more than forty years. In 2003, the present Company 
was incorporated and, in January 2005, took over the 
assets and liabilities of the proprietorship.
From those beginnings, ACJKEL has grown into an 
integrated processor, marketer and exporter of basmati 
and specialty rice, with a presence in 38 countries 
across four continents and a portfolio that now extends 
into everyday kitchen fmcg staples products like atta, 
besan ,sooji, maida ,salt and sugar. Going forward, Amir 
Chand Jagdish Kumar will leverage its established 
sales and logistics partner network to accelerate its 
expansion into the FMCG sector.
In FY 2025–26, India contributed 
67.28 per cent of consolidated 
revenue from operations
(Rs. 15,387.41 million out of
Rs. 22,871.38 million), reflecting 
the deliberate strategic 
repositioning to capture India’s 
rapid shift toward branded 
consumer staples.
Three Star Export House by the Ministry of Commerce 
and Industry. We also rank third among our listed peers 
by revenue on the CARE report we commissioned.
What has carried the business this far is not a single 
advantage but the accumulation of several, all 
supporting that same thesis. We work the whole value 
chain ourselves, from procurement through storage, 
ageing, milling, processing, packing, branding and 
distribution, which very few Indian rice companies do. 
We buy through 325 registered agents across Punjab, 
Haryana, Delhi and Uttar Pradesh, a network held 
together over four decades by consistent offtake and 
payments made when they fall due. And we protect 
what we have built: 100 registered trademarks, 70 in 
India and 30 across 26 countries, together with 22 
copyrights.
The domestic market represents the core driver of 
Company performance. In FY 2025–26, India contributed 
67.28 per cent of consolidated revenue from operations 
(Rs. 15,387.41 million out of Rs. 22,871.38 million), 
reflecting the deliberate strategic repositioning to 
capture India’s rapid shift toward branded consumer 
staples.
During the year, the Company completed its Initial 
Public Offering (IPO), issuing 2,07,54,716 equity shares 
of face value Rs. 10 at a premium of Rs. 202 (issue 
price Rs. 212 per share), raising gross proceeds of Rs. 
4,400.00 million (Rs. 440.00 crore). The equity shares 
were listed on NSE and BSE on April 02, 2026. The 
proceeds significantly strengthened net worth to Rs. 
9,241.32 million and are being deployed for working 
capital augmentation.
The years ahead should take the same business further 
rather than turn it into a different one: wider distribution, 
particularly in tier 3 and tier 4 towns; greater investment 
in a brand that has done remarkably well on very little 
advertising; a larger share of the kitchen beyond rice; 
and fuller use of plants that are today running at a little 
over half their capacity.
Product Range
Our portfolio is organised by grade. Grade is the central 
organising principle: it determines the price tier, which 
determines the brand, and the brand determines the 
pack, the price and the channel it goes into. More than 
40 sub-brands fall under the Aeroplane mark, allowing 
us to compete across the price curve without weakening 
the premium end.
The Company will utilize its existing distribution reach, 
market relationships and logistics capabilities to 
efficiently introduce and scale its FMCG portfolio.
This asset-light approach will enable faster market 
penetration, wider geographic coverage and improved 
supply-chain efficiency.
By partnering with experienced channel partners, 
the Company aims to build a scalable, cost-efficient 
and sustainable FMCG business across India and 
international markets.
This growth rests on a simple thesis: a broad rice 
business built for scale and reach, recognised as a

ACJKEL’s Philosophy: Trust, Earned One Harvest at a 
Time
Basmati is one of the few grains in the world worth 
more for having waited. In our trade, ageing settles 
the moisture and alters the starch within the grain, 
producing the length, the separation in the pot, and the 
aroma that a premium buyer is paying for. Nothing about 
it can be hurried. Our premium grades are aged twelve 
to twenty-four months, and our medium grades three to 
nine; no commercial pressure has ever been allowed to 
shorten either.
Trust is built the same way, and we have taken the same 
view of it: it is earned over years, one consignment and 
one household at a time, until a name on a pack means 
something before that pack is opened.
This has practical consequences for how we run the 
business: we pay our procurement agents on the day 
payment falls due, every season, in a trade that does 
not always work that way, and that reputation reaches 
the mandi before our buyers do. We check every 
consignment before it leaves our premises through a 
quality assurance team of 14 and conduct in-process 
sampling right through the mill. We register our 
marks in 26 countries because, in a category where 
counterfeiting is a real risk, a registered mark is what 
stands between a household’s confidence and a pack 
with no right to it.
We would rather be reliable than remarkable. That is 
our central standard. Excellence in this business is not 
a claim made on a pack. It is the same grain behaving 
the same way in the same kitchen, season after season, 
for forty years.
New Launch
Two additions during the period reflect a single shift 
in consumer preference. Aeroplane Diabetic Rice 
addresses a segment growing at 6 to 8 per cent globally 
and around 10 per cent in India, where households want 
a staple that fits a medical requirement without asking 
them to give up the meal they know. Aeroplane Quick 
Cook answers the same shift in a different setting: the 
urban kitchen with less time.
Both sit within our speciality range, and both are sold 
under the Aeroplane mark rather than a new one, which 
is deliberate. The choice reinforces one message: a 
household that has trusted the name for a generation 
should not have to learn a second one to buy from 
us. The same reasoning applies to our FMCG range, 
launched in 2022 and now covering atta, maida, besan, 
sooji, idli rice flour, instant phirni, salt and sugar.
Going forward, the Company will further leverage 
its existing supply-chain and distribution network to 
expand its FMCG portfolio beyond its current product 
categories. The Company plans to introduce products 
such as Ghee, Daliya, Poha and Cooking Oils, utilizing 
its established sourcing, warehousing, logistics and 
distribution capabilities.
This strategy will enable the Company to launch new 
products efficiently while minimizing incremental 
infrastructure and distribution costs.
By building on its existing ecosystem, the Company 
aims to create a diversified FMCG portfolio with strong 
cross-selling opportunities and wider consumer reach.
Manufacturing Facilities
Our units sit within the basmati belt, where they need to 
be. Punjab, Haryana and Uttar Pradesh together account 
for over 97 per cent of India’s basmati production. That 
proximity supports the same core objective: it lets our 
buyers move early in the season, shortens the distance 
the crop travels and keeps supply steady through the 
peak months of September to January.

Unit I runs automated machinery imported from Japan, 
Germany and the United States; Unit II runs machinery 
imported from Japan. The line covers pre-cleaning, 
drying, a second pre-clean, de-stoning, de-husking, 
milling and whitening, fine polishing, colour sorting, 
length grading and automated packing. What comes out 
of it is less breakage, better yield per tonne of paddy, 
and grain with consistent size, texture, and colour. That 
is the core advantage . Both processing units maintain 
their own paddy and rice laboratories, and a customised 
food grain ERP handles sales, purchases, inventory, and 
financial reporting across all three units.
Building on that quality focus, every unit holds a Central 
FSSAI licence, operates an ISO 22000:2018-compliant 
food safety management system, and is HACCP-
accredited. FMCG packing is done only at Unit III, in an 
enclosed dust-free facility on automated lines.
On the energy side, Amritsar generates its own clean 
power, with 997 kW of solar and a 950 kW biomass co-
generation turbine. Husk collected during cleaning fires 
the boilers and generates power at Unit I, which is why 
we buy no coal for steam there. Bran goes to edible oil 
extraction and cattle feed units. Process water at Units 
I and II is treated and returned to the process rather 
than drawn fresh. These systems support efficient, self-
contained operations. 
The Company’s Amritsar unit is a registered Carbon 
Green unit with the United Nations Framework 
Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC), reflecting its 
focus on sustainable and environmentally responsible 
operations.
The unit is focused on creating value from agricultural 
and biomass resources while promoting resource 
efficiency, waste-to-value creation and reduction of 
carbon emissions.
Going forward, the Company intends to further leverage 
the unit to support its sustainability objectives and 
develop additional green-business opportunities.
We closed the year using a little over half our installed 
capacity. That is the figure worth noting, because it 
means several years of volume growth can be absorbed 
within the plants we already own. The central point is 
that our existing capacity still leaves room to grow.

growth of 6.69 per cent. Realisation per tonne increased 
by approximately 8.07 per cent, indicating that both 
higher volumes and improved realisation contributed to 
revenue growth. In a category where the trade passes 
price increases straight back, this combination provides 
a balanced basis for growth.
FMCG revenue increased 34.28 per cent, from Rs. 
43.15 million to Rs. 57.94 million. We give both figures

MARKET REVIEW
Building a Global FMCG Footprint
International markets have been an integral part of our 
journey, with the Company exporting Basmati rice for 
most of its operating history. Today, this international 
platform provides us with a strong foundation as we 
pursue our ambition of becoming a full-fledged FMCG 
company with a growing portfolio of branded food 
products. We are recognised as a Three-Star Export 
House by the Ministry of Commerce and Industry and 
currently serve 38 countries across four continents 
through 53 international distributors, reflecting the 
breadth of our overseas distribution network.
Our international strategy is increasingly focused on 
building long-term markets and consumer franchises, 
rather than relying on individual transactions. We have 
registered our trademarks in 26 countries, providing a 
foundation for building and protecting our brands as 
we expand our product portfolio across geographies. 
The Middle East remains our oldest and one of our 
most established international markets, where Indian 
Basmati is a widely consumed staple and several 
of our customer relationships extend over multiple 
decades with recurring orders. We also have a presence 
across Australia, South Africa, Europe, Azerbaijan and 
other markets, and continue to explore opportunities 
to deepen our penetration across existing and new 
geographies.
Our expanding Indian footprint provides us with an 
important platform for taking branded FMCG products 
Pan- India and later beyond India. As we broaden 
our domestic portfolio, we believe our existing 
international relationships, distributor network and 
market knowledge can provide an efficient route for 
introducing additional categories in overseas markets. 
This creates the potential to leverage the same brand, 
sourcing, quality and supply-chain capabilities across 
multiple products and geographies.
FMCG revenue increased 34.28 
per cent, from Rs. 43.15 million 
to Rs. 57.94 million. We give 
both figures rather than the 
percentage alone, since on a 
base this small the percentage

Our export operations are supported by an integrated dispatch and logistics function working closely with our sales 
team. Depending on customer and market requirements, consignments may be dispatched from our manufacturing 
facilities to ICDs or loaded directly into shipping containers at Mundra, Nhava Sheva or other Indian ports, based on 
logistics efficiency. We undertake sales on Ex-Works, FOR, FOB, CIF and DDP terms, depending on the requirements of 
individual customers and markets.
Our manufacturing and regulatory capabilities also support our ability to serve demanding international markets. Our 
relevant mill registrations in the United States and China, together with EIC approval for Unit II, provide access to 
markets with stringent product and regulatory requirements. Going forward, we intend to use this established export 
and domestic infrastructure not only for Basmati rice but also as a scalable distribution platform for our broader FMCG 
portfolio.
Our vision is therefore to evolve from being an established rice exporter into a multi-category, multi-channel FMCG 
company with a strong domestic consumer franchise and a diversified global footprint.

The rest of the world includes Australia, South Africa, 
Europe, Azerbaijan, and other markets. Export markets 
numbered 38 at the close of the year, against 37 a year 
earlier.
 
Export Market Brands
Our export packs are designed to meet the needs of each 
market while keeping the same core architecture as our 
domestic ones. Premium grades travel under Aeroplane 
La-Taste, Classic, Super, 1121, Gold, Select and Royal, 
and under Ali Baba, which has particular recognition in 
the Gulf. Bulk and institutional requirements are served 
by the HORECA tier, Jet, Aeroplane Power, Speed, Shan 
and Race, in the fifty-kilogram and larger formats that 
commercial kitchens buy in. 
Key Customers
Our products are increasingly positioned to reach end 
consumers through a diversified and scalable multi-
channel distribution model, comprising General Trade 
(GT), distributors and retailers, our own e-commerce 
platform and quick-commerce channels. This approach 
enables us to expand consumer reach, improve product 
accessibility and participate across both traditional and 
rapidly evolving digital consumption channels.
Our strategy is to leverage our established sales and 
logistics ecosystem while progressively strengthening 
our direct consumer presence. The combination 
of an extensive physical distribution network with 
e-commerce and quick-commerce platforms provides 
us with multiple routes to market, reduces reliance 
on any single channel and enables us to respond 
more effectively to changing consumer purchasing 
behaviour.
We also serve institutional customers, including 
hotels, restaurants and catering companies, hospitals, 
flight kitchens and other bulk buyers. Going forward, 
we intend to deepen our presence across consumer 
and institutional markets and leverage our existing 
infrastructure to introduce and scale additional FMCG 
categories, thereby creating opportunities for cross-
selling, higher consumer engagement and broader 
portfolio penetration.
A total distributor base of 489 across domestic and 
international markets keeps us from depending on any 
one of them.
Institutional customers are served directly by our own 
sales team rather than through distributors. These 
are hotels, restaurants and catering companies, 
hospitals, flight kitchens and other bulk buyers. What 
they need is not a name on a shelf but the same grain 
behaving the same way in the same kitchen every time, 
and supplying hospitals and flight kitchens carries a 
standard of care we take seriously.
MARKET REVIEW
Domestic
India is now our largest market and the centre of 
our plans. It contributed 67.28 per cent of revenue 
from operations this year, up 24.54 per cent from Rs. 
12,355.82 million to Rs. 15,387.41 million. The increase 
in domestic revenue was the principal contributor to the 
overall growth in revenue from operations.
Unlike much of the branded rice trade, we did not build 
the domestic business on a new brand. Aeroplane 
was already there, already asked for by name across 
a counter, so the work was to put it in front of more 
households in more places. We are present across the 
metros and most tier-1 and tier-2 cities through three 
channels.
General trade distributors are selected based on area 
coverage, sales capability, financial and operating 
record, market standing, and credit practices. There are 
no long-term contracts, but many of these associations 
run for years, and their depth allows us to hold finished 
stock at the distributor end rather than at our own.
Modern trade runs through arrangements with national 
and regional retail chains, supported by in-store 
promotion and joint activity. This is where the premium 
tier competes most directly on shelf, and where pack, 
grade proposition and price ladder do most of the 
selling.
E-commerce and quick commerce, together with our own 
website which has been live since 2018, account for less 
than a percent of revenue. That is small. But it is also the 
only place where we deal with the consumer directly, at 
our own price, and can see exactly who is buying what, 
and we intend to make a good deal more of it.
Domestic Product Range
The domestic portfolio runs the full width of the 
Aeroplane architecture: premium, medium, value and 
HORECA tiers in basmati, a speciality range covering 
kolam, idli rice, sona masoori, diabetic rice, brown rice 
and quick cook, and the FMCG staples line of atta, 
maida, besan, sooji, idli rice powder, instant phirni, salt 
and sugar.
The logic of the range is that a household buying 
aged premium basmati for a wedding and a caterer 
buying fifty-kilogram sacks for daily service are not the 
same customer and should not be offered the same 
pack.,spices ,masala ghee, daliya, poha Pulses and 
cooking oils are intended to follow, with value-added 
lines, including honey, pasta, and rice noodles, after 
those.
Robust Distribution and Supplier Network
Two networks hold this business together, one at each 
end.
On the supply side, 325 registered procurement agents 
work the mandis of Punjab, Haryana, Delhi and Uttar 
Pradesh on our behalf, alongside our own buying team. 
We hold no long-term contract with any of them. What 
holds the network together is four decades of consistent 
offtake and payment on the day it falls due, which, 
in a trade where the better lots move within hours of 
arriving, is worth more than a contract would be.
On the demand side, 436 distributors operate in India 
and 53 abroad, a total of 489, up by 14 in the year. We 
have said publicly that we intend to be past 700 in 
India by the close of Fiscal 2028, which will require 
appointments at a pace well above this year’s, with 
most of them in tier-3 and tier-4 towns. We made that 
commitment knowing we would be measured against it.

our strategy of building Aeroplane into a strong 
and aspirational FMCG brand across domestic and 
international markets.

Ratios moving more than 25 per cent
• 
Current ratio increased 36.44 per cent, from 1.25 to 1.70, primarily due to IPO proceeds outstanding in the monitoring/
escrow account at year-end. Excluding the IPO proceeds, the current ratio would have been 1.50.
• 
Debt-to-equity ratio declined 60.33 per cent, from 2.07 to 0.82, primarily due to additional capital introduced during 
the year at premium.
• 
Net capital turnover ratio declined 48.51 per cent, from 7.92 to 4.08, primarily due to IPO proceeds outstanding in the 
monitoring/escrow account at year-end. Excluding the IPO proceeds, the ratio would have been 5.19.
• 
Net profit ratio increased 48.64 per cent, from 3.04 per cent to 4.52 per cent, reflecting improved overall profitability 
and higher sales.
• 
Return on capital employed increased 21.04 per cent, from 14.36 per cent to 17.38 per cent. This movement is below 
the 25 per cent threshold for a detailed explanation.
Risks and Concerns
The Company operates a risk framework in which risks are continuously identified, assessed, and mitigated, with 
material matters reported to the Board. Our principal exposures are set out below, with the corresponding mitigations. 
Where a risk is not fully mitigated, we have said so.
Risks
Measures
Single annual harvest — weather, pest or yield
failure would hit availability and cost together
Procurement across states; 2,90,000 MT paddy and rice 
storage covering the full season; aged inventory carrying 
supply forward
Paddy price — set by the market, and it moves 
between our purchase and our sale
Scale and timing of procurement; 325 agents; storage that 
lets us pick our week. Not fully mitigated — margin stays 
sensitive to input cost
Export routes — Middle East conflict and Red Sea
and Hormuz disruption raise freight, insurance
and transit into our largest overseas region
Forty destination countries; flexibility between Mundra and 
Nhava Sheva; a domestic book at 66.04% of revenue. The 
regional exposure itself cannot be hedged
Policy and compliance — export incentives,
minimum export prices, destination duties and 
phytosanitary rules can change without notice
Mill registrations for the USA and China; EIC approval at 
Unit II; ISO 22000:2018 and HACCP at all units; continuous 
monitoring of trade notifications
Concentration — no long-term contracts
with agents;
489 distributors across domestic and international markets; 
four decades of prompt payment and our own mandi licences; 
100 registered marks across 26 countries

Internal Control Systems and their Adequacy
The Company maintains internal controls suited to its operations, ensuring reliable financial reporting, efficient 
operations, asset protection, fraud/error prevention, accurate records, and legal compliance. A customised ERP covers 
all units, tracking sales, purchases, inventory, and finances, with a unified transaction record. Inventory control is crucial 
due to the long ageing of paddy/rice, with regular stock verification.
Internal audit reviews the effectiveness of controls and reports to the Audit Committee, which also examines financial 
statements before Board approval. The Board opines that internal financial controls were adequate and effective during 
the year.
Human Resources and Industrial Relations
We had 226 permanent and 270 contract employees. Our 43-member sales team manages distributors and institutional 
accounts, while a 14-member quality team oversees product quality across all units.
We regularly recognise employee contributions, emphasising sound judgment in quality across all levels and locations. 
There were no labour disputes or stoppages; industrial relations remained cordial.

MATERIAL CHANGES AND COMMITMENTS, IF ANY, AFFECTING THE FINANCIAL POSITION OF THE COMPANY WHICH 
HAVE OCCURRED BETWEEN THE END OF THE FINANCIAL YEAR OF THE COMPANY TO WHICH THE FINANCIAL 
STATEMENTS RELATE AND THE DATE OF THIS REPORT
No material changes other than those disclosed as part of this Annual Report, affecting the financial position of the 
Company have occurred between March 31, 2026, and the date of this Annual Report.
Further, pursuant to the approval of NSE and BSE, the trading symbol and Scrip ID (in the BOLT Plus system) of the 
Company has been changed from ‘AMIRCHAND’ to ‘AEROPLANE’, effective from July 20, 2026.
INITIAL PUBLIC OFFER AND LISTING OF COMPANY ON STOCK EXCHANGES
The Company had undertaken the requisite preparations for listing of its equity shares and, in connection therewith, 
filed the draft red herring prospectus (“DRHP”) with the respective stock exchanges, viz. National Stock Exchange of 
India Limited (“NSE”) and BSE Limited (“BSE”), on June 27, 2025.
The Company received the in-principle approvals from BSE and NSE on September 24, 2025. Subsequently, the 
Board noted the letter issued by the Securities and Exchange Board of India (“SEBI”) dated October 8, 2025, bearing 
reference number SEBI/HO/CFD/RAC-DIL3/P/OW/2025/26180/1.
Pursuant thereto, the Company filed the red herring prospectus (“RHP”) dated March 12, 2026 and the Prospectus 
dated March 28, 2026 with the Registrar of Companies, National Capital Territory of Delhi-II at Central Delhi (“RoC”) 
and SEBI, respectively.
The Company thereafter successfully completed its Initial Public Offering (“IPO”), pursuant to which it received 
funding of Rs. 4,400 Million. The issue opened for subscription on March 24, 2026, and closed on March 27, 2026, 
while the issue for anchor investors opened and closed on March 23, 2026. The allotment of securities was completed 
on March 30, 2026, in consultation with Emkay Global Financial Services Limited and Keynote Financial Services 
Limited, the Book Running Lead Managers (“BRLMs”) to the issue, and the authorised signatory of BSE, being the 
Designated Stock Exchange.
The securities of the Company were subsequently admitted for trading and became available for trading on both BSE 
and NSE with effect from April 2, 2026.
TRANSFER TO RESERVES
During the year under review, no amount was transferred to reserves by the Company.
DIVIDEND
The Board does not recommend any dividend on the equity shares of the Company for the financial year ended March 
31, 2026, considering that the Company is in growth stage and requires funds to support its growth objectives. 
The Dividend Distribution Policy, in terms of Regulation 43A of the Securities and Exchange Board of India (Listing 
Obligations and Disclosure Requirements) Regulations, 2015 (“SEBI Listing Regulations”) can be accessed at
https://www.aeroplanerice.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/05/Dividend-Policy_V1.2025-1.pdf.

NOMINATION AND REMUNERATION POLICY
The Board has, on the recommendation of the Nomination and Remuneration Committee framed a policy for selection 
and appointment of directors, key managerial personnel, senior management personnel and their remuneration. 
The Company’s remuneration policy is market-driven and aims at attracting and retaining high-performance talent. 
The Managing Director and other Executive Directors are paid remuneration by way of salary, benefits, perquisites, 
allowances (fixed component). Annual compensation changes are decided after considering external benchmark data 
and overall business performance within the salary scale approved by the Board and Shareholders.
The remuneration policy approved by the Board can be accessed at
https://www.aeroplanerice.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/06/7.-Nomination-and-Remuneration-Policy.pdf. 
DECLARATION BY DIRECTORS
The Company has received necessary declarations/ disclosures from each Independent Directors of the Company 
under Section 149(7) of the Act and Regulation 25(8) of the SEBI Listing Regulations that they fulfill the criteria of 
Independence as prescribed under Section 149(6) of the Act and Regulation 16(1)(b) of the SEBI Listing Regulations, 
and have also confirmed that they are not aware of any circumstance or situation, which exist or may be reasonably 
anticipated, that could impair or impact their ability to discharge their duties with an objective independent judgement 
and without any external influence. 
The Independent Directors have also confirmed that they have registered themselves with the Independent Director’s 
Database maintained by the Indian Institute of Corporate Affairs. All the Independent Directors have qualified in the 
online proficiency self-assessment test or are exempted from passing the test as required in terms of section 150 of 
the Act read with rule 6 of the Companies (Appointment and Qualifications of Directors) Rules, 2014.
None of the Directors of the Company are disqualified from being appointed as Directors as specified under Section 
164(1) and 164(2) of the Act read with Rule 14(1) of the Companies (Appointment and Qualification of Directors) 
Rules, 2014 (including any statutory modification(s) and or re-enactment(s) thereof for the time being in force) or are 
debarred or disqualified by SEBI, Ministry of Corporate Affairs (“MCA”) or any other such statutory authority.
Further, based on these disclosures and confirmations, the Board is of the opinion that the Directors of the Company 
are distinguished persons with integrity and have necessary expertise and experience to continue to discharge their 
responsibilities as the Directors of the Company.
FAMILIARISATION PROGRAMME FOR INDEPENDENT DIRECTORS 
The Disclosure pertaining to familiarisation programme for Independent Directors is provided in the report on 
Corporate Governance Report forming part of this Annual Report.
SEPARATE MEETING OF INDEPENDENT DIRECTORS 
During the year under review, the Independent Directors met on March 13, 2026 without the presence of Non 
Independent Directors and members of the management.
BOARD MEETINGS
During the year under review, 16 (Sixteen) meetings of the Board of Directors were convened and held, the details 
of which are given in the report on Corporate Governance as Annexed to this Annual Report, which is forming a part 
of this Annual Report. The intervening gap between the said Meetings of Board of Directors was within the period 
prescribed under the Companies Act, 2013. The details of the Board Meetings and the attendance of Directors thereat, 
forms part of the Corporate Governance Report, which is annexed to this Annual Report.
COMMITTEE MEETINGS
The Board has duly constituted an Audit Committee, Nomination and Remuneration Committee, Stakeholders 
Relationship Committee, Corporate Social Responsibility Committee and Risk Management Committee in terms of 
the requirements of the Companies Act, 2013 read with the Rules made thereunder and regulations 18, 19, 20 and 21 
of the SEBI Listing Regulations.
The details of the role and composition of these Committees, including the number of meetings held during the 
financial year and attendance at these meetings are provided in the Corporate Governance section of the Annual 
Report.
ANNUAL PERFORMANCE EVALUATION OF THE BOARD
In compliance with the provisions of the Act, the Nomination and Remuneration Committee has approved the process, 
format, attributes, and criteria for the performance evaluation of the Board, Board Committees and Individual Directors 
including the Managing Director. The process provides that the performance evaluation shall be carried out on an 
annual basis. During the year, the Directors had completed the evaluation process, which included evaluation of the 
Board as a whole, the Board Committees and individual Directors including the Managing Director.
The Directors actively participated in the evaluation process.
CORPORATE SOCIAL RESPONSIBILITY (CSR) REPORT
In terms of the provisions of Section 135 of the Act, read with Companies (Corporate Social Responsibility Policy) 
Rules, 2014, and amendment thereof, the Board has constituted a Corporate Social Responsibility (CSR) Committee. 
The composition of the CSR Committee is provided in the report on Corporate Governance, which forms part of this 
Annual Report.
The Company’s CSR Projects are compliant with the CSR mandate as specified under section 135 read with Schedule 
VII of the Act along with the Companies (Corporate Social Responsibility Policy) Rules, 2014 (“CSR Rules”), as 
amended from time to time and in line with notifications issued by the MCA, from time to time.
The brief outline of CSR Policy, including overview of the program proposed to be undertaken, the composition
of the CSR Committee, average net profits of the Company for the past three financial years, prescribed
CSR expenditure and details of amount spent on CSR activities during the financial year have been disclosed
in Annexure 1 to this report, as mandated under the said Rules. Further, the Corporate Social Responsibility Policy
of the Company as approved by the Board has been hosted on the website of the Company at
https://www.aeroplanerice.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/06/2.-CSR-Policy.pdf. 
The Chief Financial Officer (CFO) of the Company has certified that the CSR funds so disbursed for the projects have 
been utilized for the purposes and in the manner as approved by the Board.
RISK MANAGEMENT
The Company has implemented a risk management framework and has in place a mechanism to inform the Board 
about risk management and minimisation procedures and periodical review to ensure that risks are controlled by the 
framework.
The main objective is to ensure sustainable business growth with stability and to promote a pro-active approach 
in reporting, evaluating and resolving risks associated with the business. In order to achieve the key objective, it 
establishes a structured and disciplined approach to manage the risk, in order to guide decisions on risk related 
issues.
	
In today’s challenging and competitive environment, strategies for mitigating inherent risks in accomplishing the 
growth plans of the Company are imperative. The common risks inter alia are regulations, competition, Business 
risk which inter-alia, includes financial risk, political risk, fidelity risk, legal risk etc., Technology obsolescence, 
Investments, retention of talent and expansion of facilities.

to the peer review process of Institute of Chartered Accountants of India (ICAI) and holds a valid certificate issued 
by the peer review Board of the ICAI.
	
Statutory Auditor’s Report
	
The Auditors’ Report on the Standalone and Consolidated Financial Statements of the Company does not contain 
any qualification, reservation or adverse remark.
	
Further, during the year under review, the Auditors had not reported any fraud matter under section 143 (12) of the 
Act in their report.

of the Company to periodically audit the adequacy and effectiveness of the internal controls laid down by the 
management. During the year, no material weakness in our operating effectiveness was observed. During the 
financial year 2025-26, the Internal Audit Report does not contain any qualification, reservation or adverse remark.
